ParlaMint-DK_20160510131431	Thank you. During these years, a very large number of refugees and family-run foreigners are coming to Denmark. We Social Democrats have therefore supported various tightenings of the immigration legislation in order to bring down that number of people. But whether we succeed or not, there is another part of our foreign policy that is at least as important, namely how we ensure that the people who are actually here become an integral part of the Danish labour market. This is important for two reasons. This is important for the sake of those people themselves. It is important for their identity, for their dignity. We Social Democrats believe that having something to stand up for in the morning, that being a contributing part of the society in which you live is important for all people. Of course, this is also important for the sake of our economy. It is important for the sake of coherence in this country that as many as possible are an active and contributing part and as few as possible on transfer. That is why we very much welcome this new educational opportunity. We believe that a so-called Igu will have a positive impact. This may be a first step into a labour market which may otherwise be difficult to address, partly because of the high qualification requirements we set in Denmark and partly because of the high level of remuneration. ParlaMint-DK_20160510133459	Thank you. In the Danish People's Party we fear that the bill will lead to wage dumping, as the proposal is really about incorporating refugees into the Danish labour market through a lower integration wage. We recognise that there has been an improvement since the conclusion of the tripartite agreement, in that there is also a subsidy for Igu students. But that does not change the basics, namely that, from our point of view, we are talking about wage dumping, and we want to counteract that. We have also noted that the second legislative proposal under discussion today introduces a separate bonus for companies employing refugees who are not part of the Igu process. This bonus will not be paid to companies if they employ Danes, which is why, when you look at the entire implementation of the two and three-way agreements, there is, overall, a discrimination against Danes, which we do not want to support from the Danish People's Party. In addition, the Danish People's Party fundamentally disagrees with the integration of refugees. We have gone away from the idea that we can succeed in creating a stable and well-functioning transition to Danish society. It would be nice if it could succeed, but all experience shows that this is unfortunately not going to happen. We've become clever with damage. We do not want to integrate foreigners with asylum status. We will help those who come to Denmark for a temporary period, and then they will have to be sent out again. The whole system should be built up to ensure that refugees are only allowed to stay in Denmark for a limited period of time. The rules in this area and the information provided to the refugees and their families should contain a clear message so that they are already aware on arrival that they are guests and will therefore be sent out of the country again. This bill does not support the repatriation of refugees. It more supports what we've tried and which we know doesn't work. More than 20 integration plans have been implemented and none of them have worked. We fear that it will also be the result of this plan and that it will not have much effect either in terms of integrating those who are here, and certainly not in terms of sending them home, which we believe should be sent home. In addition, we think it is wrong that the Igu means that you earn the right to a daily allowance. Because even if they want to integrate, everything else will have the same effect that, once they have completed the process and they have earned their right to benefit, there is an incentive for them to go on unemployment benefits instead of entering the labour market. So even if you want them to enter the labour market and be more integrated, this would be counterproductive. Against this background, I must inform you that the Danish People's Party cannot support the bill.

We have sensed the consequences if we are unable to solve the task and we have been able to see the need for a completely new approach to integration with a clear focus, and that is jobs. Because it is only fair to make a contribution to Danish society as soon as possible once you have come here. At the same time, we know that it is absolutely crucial for the integration of the individual into Danish society that you enter the labour market, that you meet the Danes, the Danish language, Danish norms and values, that you get Denmark under the skin in short. Only every other person is in employment when we measure immigrants and descendants of non-Western origin. And for the newly arrived refugees, as we now see, the figures are even more depressing. The government has just calculated that the refugee pressure will trigger a total extra bill of 6.4 billion. kr. from 2017 to 2020, so the bill will not be less in the future if we do nothing. We cannot offer this either to the hardworking Danes who are here or to the very talented companies who have created wealth. For the same reason, I am also pleased that this government saw as its first task to make it less attractive to seek asylum in Denmark and to create razor-sharp incentives for new entrants to enter the labour market. In concrete terms, I should like to mention the integration benefit as one of several major tightenings in the living conditions of new entrants. However, the figures also highlight the need for a new integration policy and that we are not just implementing more of the same, more of everything that does not work, but that we are changing the very approach to integration. This means that more of what we know is working and which has been used far too little in the past, namely getting into business. This means that we must demand something more from those who come here. In my opinion, we can contribute well, even if we do not speak fluent Danish and we have just come to the country. This also means that we should not make excessive and unnecessarily rigid demands on those who have to carry out the task, namely the municipalities and the businesses of the country. The many newcomers are creating the need for greater flexibility, as I have said, new solutions. Such a solution is the Igu, and that is what we are dealing with right now. As you know, it is proposed by DA and LO, which share the Government's ambition for a new approach. The DA and the LO therefore put on the table a joint proposal for a new two-year integration training, which will be a new and unbureaucratic stepping stone to the labour market. It is a system in which companies and refugees themselves agree on employment. The Igu means that refugees are given the opportunity to work and train themselves, even if their qualifications are not sufficient for a job at full pay. This will be possible because the wage level corresponds to the rates of internships during vocational training and because the State pays for upskilling. Companies taking Igu students receive a bonus. At the same time, the parties have attached great importance to the protection of the Igu on the Danish labour market model and against social dumping. Igu pupils are covered by the collective agreement which applies to enterprises. This applies, among other things, to: in relation to working time, repayment, notice of termination and other matters, and it is ensured that companies employing Igu pupils must consult their employees in accordance with applicable agreement or law. The government and the social partners do not see the challenges in the field of employment as either or as a question of both refugees and other groups. That is why we have also agreed on the following, and I quote from both the tripartite agreement and the comments to the bill: "It is absolutely crucial that the start-up of an IGU scheme for refugees and families does not impair other groups' opportunities for lasting association with the Danish labour market through other targeted schemes, such as: initial vocational training (egu). That is why the draft law also ensures that a bonus is introduced for companies that have employed pupils after initial vocational training, on an equal footing with the bonus proposed for the Igu. Therefore, in the further trialogue negotiations, the government has set out to discuss how we can solve the challenges of lack of internships, so that more people choose to complete vocational training. The government and the social partners are, of course, also very concerned about ensuring that the scheme becomes a reality in harmony with the good intentions that lie here. That is why we have also agreed to follow the scheme closely and evaluate it in cooperation with the parties. I hope, of course, that we will also be able to deal with this at second reading, so that we can have the proposal here adopted so that more refugees can get started on the labour market. Because, as I said, it is perfectly reasonable that when you come to Denmark, you should also contribute.

It is therefore gratifying that the social partners and the municipalities have agreed to two-party and tripartite agreements which can help to lead the way towards better and stronger integration. We Social Democrats have long argued that refugees who come to Denmark should enter the labour market as soon as possible and that, as a starting point, they should be declared ready for work from day one. We have also said that there is much to be gained by improving the screening of individual competences and by taking into account employment opportunities when talking about housing placement. We therefore welcome the fact that the tripartite and two-party negotiations resulted in a number of concrete initiatives which will respond to these proposals. We also support the implementation of a regulatory simplification, a simplification that can ensure that integration efforts can become more flexible and less bureaucratic. Overall, this is a good legislative proposal with many good, concrete initiatives. However, there are also remarks that give cause for concern in the consultation answers that have come in, things that I expect we will be dealing with in committee and which the Minister may also be motivated to say a few words about here today. One thing we Social Democrats are nervous about is that the bill unnecessarily creates barriers for the high schools to continue to play a positive role in integration in Denmark, because although we obviously believe that the best thing for the refugees is to get into work from the first day, there are certainly also cases where this will not happen. Here I believe that the high schools can be an important partner in ensuring linguistic and cultural competences, which are of course important in our society. I therefore hope that the Minister will say a few words about the role of the high schools, just as I would certainly like to address this issue in committee. Another question concerns the targeting of the health assessment. Here, too, a number of critical points have been raised in the hearings on which I would like to hear the Minister's views. We Social Democrats are also concerned that this targeting could have unintended consequences. On the whole, however, we are positive and overall support the proposal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510154839	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no more brief comments, so we go further in the rapporteur's line to Mr Beyer de Ryke. Finn Sørensen, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510154853	Thank you. Of course, the Enhedslisten agrees that it is important for refugees to be integrated as soon as possible and that participation in the labour market is an important part of this, just as the aim must also be for the refugees to get an ordinary job, which they can live off with the pleasant side benefits, so that they pay a greater tax contribution to Danish society than they do if they are on a transfer income. I do not believe that this bill will lead to a lot of refugees getting an ordinary job. That is not what is meant by it, you can see when you look down through it and if they get it, it will certainly happen at the expense of some of the 170,000 unemployed we already have. For it is true that this bill resembles all sorts of other policy from the government and, for that matter, from the previous government: what you are very concerned about is increasing this supply of labour so as to increase the supply of labour. We are not concerned with increasing the demand for labour by creating more jobs, which would otherwise give way to the labour market, not just to refugees, but to the many unemployed we already have. This is clear from the entire legislative proposal, and for that reason also from the tripartite agreement on which it is based, that the intention is that all refugees should be placed much faster than they are today in so-called business-oriented offers. But what does that mean? That sounds good, but in the real world it is a business practice, a scheme in which refugees work for up to 3 months free of charge and where one internship can replace the other. A lot of companies have already found out. In addition, this area will be tightened up so that no more than six weeks may pass between each internship. This consideration, the so-called business-oriented offers, is above all else, and this bill is based on it. It is higher than proper language teaching. It is higher than social and health interventions. These things must give way so that they are subordinated to the stated purpose, and that is why the bill is part of a massive plan to use refugees as cheap or free labour. After all, the bill must be seen in the context of the Igu bill we have just considered. It was with the cheap labor, in some places even very cheap. So here's the free labor. What is the experience of this, and where do the refugees end up under this policy? The experience is that the job effect of company-oriented offers in the form of internships is very, very small. The social workers say it's only 11%. of the courses ending with an ordinary job. At the same time, it is also in the tripartite agreement that refugees must first and foremost be taken into account in the sectors where there are job openings. But where are there many so-called job openings? That's where there's a lot of penetration. It's in the hotel and restaurant business. It's in the field of cleaning. It is in the service industry, in agriculture, in forestry, in horticulture. It is especially in the hotel and catering industry and the cleaning industry. So who's gonna meet the fugitive there? The fugitive meets a whole lot of foreigners. The majority in the hotel and catering industry in the unskilled jobs are people of a different origin from Danish. Danish is not spoken in many of the jobs; English is spoken. There's not much good integration in it. In addition, there are a number of further clear deteriorations in quality in terms of the integration of refugees. All refugees are per. definition job preparation from day one. It is only if it is obvious that you can be searched as a ready-to-work person, i.e. someone who is not ready for the labour market immediately. But these people too have an obligation to make available, according to the bill, which affects the many refugees who have war traumas and various diseases. They may be penalised, albeit at a slightly lower level than others. We are weakening the obligations to provide the refugees with a permanent accommodation. There is no longer any obligation for municipalities to carry out a health check, with the result that many physical and mental disorders and diseases of refugees are not detected and prevented and cured. Then the demand for language teaching and for the integration programme is relaxed at all. Fewer people will be able to make transport allowances. It's just to mention some of the deteriorations there are. On top of that, a completely foolish bonus system is then introduced, whereby municipalities and companies who receive refugees in ordinary jobs receive a bonus. Similarly, if a Danish unemployed person gets into work, and that is an unsolidarous scheme. All in all, the bill does not benefit integration. It is not good for integration to use refugees as free or cheap labour, and it is not good for integration to relax the requirements of health checks, language teaching, education and social and health interventions.

The refugees already live for a long time in what can be called temporary housing, and recently legislation has also relaxed the requirements for these temporary housing so that it is now possible to live under conditions that we would otherwise not offer non-refugees to live under. An uncertainty as to how long refugees can now live under so-called temporary conditions is worrying about the integration process. As I have said, this is a huge legislative proposal with a wide range of issues which, as such, are not necessarily connected, and that is a great mouthful. And I hope that the Minister can respond to some of the concerns I have raised here in the Chair so that we can get into dialogue on the things I have mentioned. ParlaMint-DK_20160510162155	There are far too many mobile phones in Danish prisons. There have been for many years. Changing ministers of justice have tried to find solutions to the problem. It is unacceptable that there are so many mobile phones in prisons. Not only is it a mockery of the victims of criminal acts, but it is also a lack of respect for our legal society. The telephones can be used to coordinate testimony, and they can also be used to threaten or press witnesses or victims, which is why it is obviously quite unreasonable that they should be smuggled in where the penalty says that you should not have a mobile phone. In the recent agreement on criminal justice, a number of initiatives were taken to prevent the smuggling of mobile phones, including: the possibility of throwing mobile phones over farm areas. But there is a need for further initiatives, which I think is clear to everyone, and that is why we welcome the proposal here. Specifically, the draft law we are discussing here aims to prevent the smuggling of telephones through the possibility of using body scanners in the institutions of the Department of Justice. Body scanners are already known from airports where both passengers and employees pass them every day. Therefore, there is nothing wild or revolutionary or unfair about including them in the criminal justice system. At the same time, compulsory disciplinary measures and exit quarantine must be introduced for inmates illegally in possession of mobile phones, and the penalties for the smuggling of mobile phones by others into inmates must be tightened up. The last part of the proposal also includes increased criminal liability for the improper inclusion of mobile phones to both visitors and other people. As I said, we support the proposal. It is quite unreasonable that inmates have phones. It is an insult to the sense of justice when they have just been sentenced to a sentence that includes not communicating with the outside world. I would also like to say that we in the Socialist Group are also prepared to look at further measures to prevent the smuggling of telephones. For example, it could be jamming, so as to prevent the use of telephones in areas where telephones are not to be carried. It could also be other new technology. However, we support the proposal that we are discussing here today.

You could say that's too many, because the inmates mustn't dodge. We could do something about it by building more closed prisons and taking all the inmates from open prisons and putting them in closed prisons. But there are also inmates who elude from closed prisons. You could do something about that too. Yes, a life sentence could be imposed for escape attempts. As most people have probably guessed, it is not initiatives supported by Enhedslisten. When we look at the problem of cell phones in prisons and detention houses, there is a difference in the seriousness of the problem, depending on whether we are talking about prison sentences or isolation prisoners. Our overall criticism of the bill is this lack of proportionality. One stares blindly at the problem of mobile phones and creates a harder everyday life for both inmates, employees and visitors in prisons and detention houses, including the many who could never dream of smuggling or using an illegal mobile phone. The proposal envisages an experimental system with body scanners, but the Danish Health Authority is very clear in its response to the fact that body scanners use X-rays and that they are potentially carcinogenic. And that makes our alarm bell ring. After all, it is not a doctor's own X-ray, but some groups of inmates, employees and visitors who are counting on repeated X-rays over a long period of time. In our view, the use of potentially carcinogenic X-rays is completely disproportionate. A possible path to go could be the use of jammers, as has also been mentioned earlier, which disrupt the mobile signal in a limited area. But that's not what the bill is about. In principle, we believe that we are trying to solve a problem which is serious but which has been created by few, by hitting many. Therefore, Enhedslisten cannot support the bill.

For us, it has been crucial that a solution was found so that journalists, relief workers and others do not run the risk of being prosecuted. With this proposal, you will have to apply for entry to the countries where the entry ban applies, and we think that is fair. The proposal will make it easier to punish persons entering or staying in a conflict zone, for example. to participate in an armed conflict for a terrorist organisation. It can of course be difficult today to lift the burden of proof that you have taken part in terrorist training. In fact, that is the problem that our public prosecutor often faces. Failure to comply with the entry ban and the residence ban will in future be punishable by a fine or imprisonment up to six years. It is clear that when such major legislative steps are taken, they give substance to consideration. Does legal certainty go hand in hand with security? We fully admit that we are introducing far-reaching legislative measures here, but we also believe that this is necessary in the fight against the too many young people who travel to Syria, for example, and train in the service of terrorism. It has already cost too many innocent lives that extreme groups have fought our democracy here in Europe. What we can do to stop recruitment for terrorist training must therefore be done. In the Social Democratic Party, we support the fight against terrorism. We want to make the life of extreme groupings as difficult as possible, and that is why we can support the proposal as it stands here.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510171540	Thank you. In the Enhedslisten we see two main problems with this bill. Firstly, in this case, there is also the government's strong belief that sharpened penalties are the solution. Secondly, there are the major problem of demarcation. It is, in fact, both geographical and legal. The problem of demarcation is raised by the provision to prohibit people from staying in a particular country or region. We do not believe that an increase in the criminal framework would act as a deterrent to the group of people who are tempted to travel to, for example, the United Kingdom. Syria for fighting for the Islamic State. As Professor Jørn Vestergaard points out in his hearing answer, all available knowledge of the most inflamed foreign fighters does not allow themselves to be intimidated by the threat of punishment. Young people's urge to fight for a cruel Islamist terrorist movement is not diminished by the threat of tougher penalties. That is also quite good logic. You could imagine that there were other things that seemed more deterrent. Of course, people who commit acts of terrorism must be punished. They will be today. However, we do not believe that tightening the penalties will solve any problems. A long, cool move is needed to make young men and women feel like part of Danish society. In the short term, we need more dialogue-oriented initiatives such as the world-famous Aarhus model, where East Jutland Police and Aarhus Municipality have been able to convince potential foreign fighters in close dialogue with the young people, their families and their networks that they do best to stay home in Aarhus. The second main problem in the draft law is the problem of demarcation and the relationship with human rights. The hearings contain a number of objections of a human nature. The draft law runs the risk of being at odds with freedom of expression, as it remains unclear when a journalist will be allowed to enter a conflict area. The Institute of Human Rights basically questions whether Denmark can make it a criminal offence to live in another state. The draft law gives the Government and the Minister for Justice what the comments call a broad discretionary power to decide which areas should be subject to entry bans, which organisations should be defined as terrorist organisations and which persons should be exempted from entry bans because they travel for a recognised purpose. We do not have good experience of leaving this kind of broad discretionary power to governments and authorities. For example: In at least one case, the Copenhagen Police and PET have taken the pass from a Dane who would fight for the Kurdish forces against the Islamic State. This was done on the basis of the amendment to the Passport Act which entered into force on 1 March 2015. In that case, the new passport law and the authorities provided estimates that the Islamic State was assisted in its terror. Thanks to the new passport law, IS had an opponent less on the battlefield. So, with the general concerns we have about this proposal, we cannot support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510172913	Thank you to the Liberal Alliance rapporteur. The next speaker is Mrs Josephine Fock as rapporteur for the alternative. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510172921	Thank you. We are dealing here with Bill No. 187 on the amendment of the Penal Code and the repeal of the Law prohibiting the support of warring Powers from the Danish Territory. The draft law contains three main points: firstly, an increase in the penal framework for engaging in terrorism or, moreover, the promotion of the activities of terrorist organisations by joining an armed force; secondly, the introduction of the possibility of administratively prohibiting entry and residence in certain areas of conflict without the prior permission of the Danish authorities; and thirdly, the introduction of a new penal provision for receiving financial support from a terrorist organisation. I think it is important to look at the legislation that is, moreover, in this area when we are discussing this. Under the Passport Act, passports may be withdrawn and travel bans imposed on Danish nationals participating in activities which may endanger the security of the State, the security of other States or a significant threat to public order. Then there is the Law on Defence Intelligence, where you can intervene in the secret of communication for those who participate in activities that may involve a terrorist threat against Denmark and Danish interests. Then there is the penal code, where you can sentence up to 10 years in prison to the one who, in an armed conflict to which Denmark is a party, joins an armed force fighting against the Danish state. Finally, there is this bill where we want to ban entry into a country or an area where a terrorist group is a party to an armed conflict. And it must be punishable by up to six years in prison. I think that the draft law opens up a number of questions to which I would very much like answers before I actually take a position on what I think about it. Because I think we all agree that we should not go out and fight and participate in terrorism. I don't think anyone in this room disagrees with that. The first problem I see in this is that it is a very large number of countries and areas in which the Minister of Justice can prohibit entry and residence. It is assumed that 39 armed conflicts existed in 24 different states in 2013. And what exactly are some areas in which entry and residence bans can be announced? It is not very precise and I think it should be formulated more precisely. That is why it is important for me to clarify what the conditions are for us to be able to grant entry and residence bans in any given area. Then there is the right of a citizen to enter his own country, which we will also restrict with this. It can be part of both a state and the whole state, and there I think we have a problem if we refuse citizens to travel to their own country. Is that really what this is all about? Then there is the whole question of whether we in Denmark can really restrict freedom of movement in other countries. At least that's something new. According to the protocol on human rights, people have the right to move freely and to choose their own place of residence. So are we quite sure that it is in line with human rights obligations if we restrict the freedom of movement of individuals living in other states? Then there is the whole area of the exercise of Danish, foreign or international public service. Then you can go in, but what is it actually quite concrete? There are also a number of different consultation responses in the area which questions what is actually included in it. Then there is the question of whether Danish courts have jurisdiction in other countries. At least that's something new. This is not something we have seen at first sight. Then there is the matter of getting financial support from a terrorist organisation abroad. It is about the receipt of money and services from a group or association which commits or intends to commit terrorist acts. What exactly is involved in being a group or a group? So I am going to ask a number of questions during the committee reading and would really like some answers to them before the alternative takes a position on this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510174013	Then we would like to thank the rapporteur for the alternative. We move on to the next speaker, and that is Mrs Zenia Stampe as rapporteur for the Radical Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510174020	Thank you. The draft law we are debating consists of three parts, of which we can support two, but are sceptical, but also open to discussion on the third point. We are in favour of increasing the penal framework in order to enlist a terrorist organisation and join an armed force. This is one of the most serious crimes that can be committed in this country, and of course we also think that the penalties should reflect this. We are also in favour of introducing a new penal code for receiving financial support from a terrorist organisation, but we believe that it should be clarified what is meant by a group or association that commits or intends to commit terrorism. We do not think that the answer given by the Ministry of Justice following the hearing from the Institute for Human Rights is entirely satisfactory, because that answer means that there will be cases where clarification can only be obtained through legal proceedings. Then perhaps the most far-reaching proposal is to introduce an entry ban on certain areas of conflict without prior authorisation. Such a ban will make it possible to come after the holy warriors whom we have had difficulty prosecuting so far, because it has not been possible to lift the burden of proof in relation to the criminal code's terrorist provision or the provision of treason. And that's good. Because I think there are many of us who have been frustrated to know that people have travelled to Syria and with very, very high probability have participated in holy war, but that we did not have the opportunity to prosecute them. The possibility offers such a provision, of course. However, we should not imagine that an entry ban would deter holy warriors from going to conflict areas or prevent them from doing so. This makes it possible to prosecute them, even when we cannot prove that they have been involved in holy wars, so we can just prosecute them for going to a conflict area ^ but that will hardly prevent them from going. So, overall, we are positive, but we have three concerns where we may be able to be met by some clarifications. Firstly, there is the need for access for Danish journalists and humanitarian staff to carry out their work in conflict areas. The proposal provides for the possibility of granting authorisation, even collective and prior authorisation, to large media and organisations. But it is a reality that most foreign journalists work as freelancers, and we therefore believe that consideration should be given to whether a general exemption rule can be made for example. members of the Danish Journalist Federation. We consider the risk of abuse of such a rule to be relatively limited. Then we think that there is an equal amount of rubber band in the penal framework. It is clear from the comments that it is usually only a few months' imprisonment to violate this entry ban, but you can get a sentence of up to 6 years in serious cases. The question then is whether these serious cases, where the burden of proof can also be lifted, will not really be covered by the criminal code on counter-terrorism and treason. In any case, we would like it to be made clear what aggravating circumstances make the penalty not only to be measured for months but this year. Finally, one issue has been raised by several people up here: how to take the decision to grant entry bans for an area or a country. There have been several people up here today who have said that there are 39 armed conflicts in 24 countries right now, so the entry ban can potentially be applied in a very, very large number of areas and countries. We therefore believe that it should be clarified, in addition to the fact that there is an armed conflict in this area, what else can be done to grant entry bans. Then we also want an administrative decision by the Minister to be reversed, for example. Here in the Folketing, which I know the Liberal Alliance will propose we will be very positive about. So, to sum up, we are in favour of increasing the penal framework in order to join a terrorist organisation and an armed force. We are in favour of introducing a penalty for receiving funds from a terrorist organisation, but we want clarification of what such an organisation really is. We can also support an entry ban if there is easy access for journalists, not least freelancers, and if it is clarified what are aggravating circumstances, then we are talking about punishment not only for months but this year, and if there is therefore also an opportunity to reverse an administrative decision by the Minister for Justice to grant entry bans in a country and the criteria for this in general are clarified. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510174553	I believe that all parties in the Folketing agree with the challenges we face with those who have travelled to, for example. Syria and Iraq have returned again. That is why, as the House has been able to listen to the questions I have put, I also have a fundamental problem with the drafting or non-drafting of this draft law, namely the much-enhanced power that we give to a minister who is given a government by the Folketing. It is much extended powers to assess which countries and which organisations should be included in this. I simply do not think that Parliament should give so much decision-making power. The only other country that, according to the Criminal Code, has introduced something similar to this is Australia, and they have introduced a much more restrictive model. As far as I know, there are two areas on the list. It is Raqqa in Syria and Mosul in Iraq that we must not go to, and it is Parliament that is in charge if it is to change. So this is an extremely comprehensive and far-reaching legislative proposal. It is not to underestimate the task that we have as parliamentary politicians, in order to also provide security to citizens. The only question is whether this is what it takes and what the price is to do it. The European Convention on Human Rights says that interference with freedom of movement, which is a fundamental right, can be okay if it is based on legislation and if it has a recognised purpose such as the security of the state and the kingdom or the security of the citizens. However, this requires • and this is what has emerged through the practice of the Court of Human Rights • that the law should be clear, predictable and inconceivable for citizens. You need to know what you have to comply with. And there I am missing from this bill some clear definitions and a greater degree of legal certainty. Because, as I said, with this legislation, we put a blank cheque in the hands of the government, which decides where the law should apply. It seems quite arbitrary whether it is about Syria, Nigeria, Israel, Turkey, India or one of the many other places where you could say that there is an armed conflict and where there is an organisation on a terrorist list. I have talked mostly about the proposal to prohibit the admission and residence of certain areas of conflict, because I think it is the most important and far-reaching aspect of the proposal. We could agree to the introduction of a new criminal code to receive financial support from a terrorist organisation and, in principle, to an increase in the criminal framework. But we're not here to make symbol politics. We are here to make policy to remedy the problem we are facing. And these people who go out to fight for, for example. ISIS, then, doesn't wonder if they get 6 or 8 years in prison at home in Denmark. I know that Special Branch and the police say that they would be able to come after people more easily if it were the entry ban itself that they had violated, because it can be difficult to prove whether or not you have done something illegal in the country to which you have gone. I understand that very well, and it is a serious task. I'm not standing up here ignoring that. I'm just asking what the price of that is. And we will be the only country in the world, at least the only democratic country to introduce such far-reaching powers. So on this basis, and if there are no fundamental changes to the legislative proposal, we cannot support it.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510181043	Thank you for that, President, and thank you to the Enhedslisten, the Alternative and the SF for tabling motion for a resolution No. 151. The resolution calls on the government to introduce limit values for the content of heavy metals in agricultural manure and for degassed biomass from biogas plants. They justify the proposal with the accumulation and environmental risk assessed and predicted in connection with studies conducted by Aarhus University. This is not the first time we have discussed this issue. The former minister has been in consultation on the matter and answered a large number of questions. There is a clear strategy in this area, as we have indicated and with which I agree. I would like to take this opportunity today to summarise this strategy and update the Folketing in relation to the latest developments. The government, like the proposers, is aware of the development of the zinc and copper content of agricultural land. Studies in 2015 show that we do not have an environmental emergency problem, but that the current spread of zinc in particular cannot continue unchanged for many years without causing problems with the accumulation of agricultural land. The government and the industry have therefore launched a large number of studies so that we can find the right solutions in terms of reducing the strain of zinc and copper in the longer term. In particular, interested parties can see an overview of all the initiatives in the answer to Question No. 466, alm. Part, to the Danish Parliament's Committee on the Environment and Food. In relation to the request for limit values on manure as proposed, I fully agree with what was stated in the last consultation on the same matter. Here the former minister said the following, and I quote: "The content of copper and zinc in the manure depends on the content of copper and zinc in the feed. The Danish Food Authority has a relatively good knowledge of the concentration of copper and zinc in the feed, including the zinc that is given to piglets as a veterinary medicinal product. This varies only to a limited extent between different holdings, with the same type of production, e.g. the production of piglets or pigs for fattening. The content of copper and zinc in sewage sludge cannot be similarly predicted and will vary to a greater extent in an unpredictable way. Therefore, it is also required that waste be investigated for heavy metals and environmental foreign substances before it is added to a manure-based biogas plant or distributed directly on agricultural land. Therefore, a separate requirement for analysis of manure is not necessary to the same extent. ▪ I can supplement this and inform you that limit values for heavy metals in manure will result in a heavy bureaucracy of measurements and monitoring of measurements. In this context, the Government cannot agree to the motion for a resolution. It is not because there is no problem, but because it is the wrong solution. The European Commission has recently proposed adjustments to the MRLs for zinc in feed for different types of animal. For pigs for fattening, a reduction of 20% has been proposed. of the limit for zinc, while the limit values for zinc, among others, are not altered. sows and piglets. If the proposal is adopted, it will probably lead to a slightly lower consumption of zinc for animal feed and hence a slightly smaller content of zinc in manure from pig production. The proposal is expected to be put to the vote on 23 to 24 May 2016. The proposal does not concern the use of zinc oxide for the management of diarrhoea in piglets; it is to be hoped that alternative solutions will be found in the long term. It would also be good if, in the light of a closer knowledge of the physiological needs of the animals for copper, the limit values for copper in feed could be reduced without compromising the health and welfare of the animals. At the moment, we must first deal with the particularly problematic manure with high zinc and copper content. As indicated in the context of the consultation and question No. 206 in connection with the environmental approval of livestock farming, the municipalities have the possibility of setting conditions to avoid significant environmental impacts, and the Danish Environmental Protection Agency has submitted proposals for such possible assessments. Since then, new summaries of products have been prepared in connection with the use of zinc oxide as a medicinal product in piglets' productions in the light of the opinion of the Veterinary Committee of the European Medicines Agency, which contains fairly precise conditions and special precautions for the management of manure if zinc oxide is used. For example, a maximum of 40% may be applied. the manure applied on the farm is manure from treated pigs. It is necessary for the environmental approval of livestock farming to include those requirements in the procedure. The Danish Environmental Protection Agency is on its way with a guide to the municipalities, which they can use for the environmental approval of livestock farming in relevant cases. For other existing farms with piglets using zinc-containing medicinal products, both the Danish Food Safety Authority and the Danish Environmental Protection Agency are in the process of finding solutions for both the use of zinc-containing medicinal products and the handling of slurry from medicated piglets. In relation to effects on the aquatic environment, measurements of zinc and copper are included in the national monitoring programme for the aquatic environment and nature, NOVANA. Thus, zinc and copper are already measured in streams affected by either wastewater or agriculture. I therefore expect that the problems of zinc and copper, both in the short and long term, can be dealt with appropriately without unnecessarily large measurement and control programmes as proposed in this resolution.

This is because zinc and copper are added to pig feed, and this is because zinc is very much used in piglets when they get diarrhea. We also know why so many piglets suffer from diarrhea. This is because the pressure of highly intensive pig production means that the piglets are taken very, very early from their mother before their stomachs are ready for it. Then they get sick because they no longer get food from their mother, but get solid food. Then they get diarrhea, and then you treat them with zinc. We are also reasonably well aware of the possible negative effects on nature and the environment. Senior researcher John Jensen from Aarhus University, who has been responsible for this DCE report, which started the whole debate back in October 2015, says very clearly that it is not sustainable to continue to add as much zinc and copper as it is now. The metals will eventually affect the soil environment and potentially damage the earth's functionality and biodiversity. Zinc and copper accumulated in the soil cannot be removed again. That is also what the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party said. It is important that agriculture restricts the use of zinc. So we know very well the problems of nature, wild animals and human health. What we want to do with this motion for a resolution is to start dealing with this problem; to follow closely how it develops; to keep an overview of how high concentrations are in the manure and where it is located; and to follow developments in agricultural land so that we closely follow this growing problem. Then we want a limit value to be introduced, which is already available in relation to sludge, and we want it to be transferred to manure in order to solve this problem. It is clear, then, that we will have to intervene in pig production, and that is the third point in our motion for a resolution, namely that in this context we will have a transitional arrangement so that this proposal is not used overnight, but ensures that farmers have a reasonable time to adapt, that we have to look after our agricultural land and that we have to take care of the fertility of agricultural land. It is because we have a livestock production in which piglets are weaning so early that they simply cannot cope. So to counteract diarrhea and other things, you add this. But it is, after all, symptom treatment that shows that we have agricultural production that is not sustainable. We have invested in some housing systems, because a majority in parliament has also not told farmers in time that we have to have a different agricultural production, and that is why we cannot have a system where piglets should be taken from so early that they are not viable or that they become very ill and need antibiotics. Then you turned to having so much focus on antibiotic resistance that you started using more and more zinc and copper, and this is the very, very violent increase we have seen over the last 10 years. And it ends up in nature, it ends up in our watercourses, it is a future threat to public health, and we cannot continue to wait and accept that the answer we receive is that more studies need to be carried out. We know, as all the experts say, that a very large proportion of the heavy metals added to pig production end up in the manure that ends up in the fields. And we cannot get rid of it again; it accumulates and becomes a bigger and bigger problem. The main conclusions of the DCE report say, among other things, that the supply of zinc and copper together with the pig gilt has led to a significant increase in the concentration on the areas where the manure has been placed and that a continuation of current practice can lead to a significant increase in the proportion of areas where the no-effect level, i.e. this limit value established by the EU for zinc, is exceeded even if the level is already high. The metals can end up in food, they can end up in the aquatic environment, and it creates antibiotic resistance. There's too much zinc at 228,000 hectares. It's an area almost the size of Funen. I think these numbers speak for themselves. Some have tried to suggest from up here that we do not know enough, and thus implicitly admit that we do not know whether heavy metals are really harmful to humans, nature and animals. It was also said that we do not know how much is out there. Yes, we know that, because we have asked a large number of parliamentary questions, and we have received an answer to that. We know how much is being spent in agriculture and how much of what ends up in the fields. We also know that we have a fixed limit on sewage sludge. One can discuss whether it is too high, but that is why it makes no sense to say, as I unfortunately slightly heard the DF's rapporteur do, that because you have a limit on sewage sludge and thus implicitly recognise that you do not have a zero limit, we cannot set a limit on manure. I don't think that makes any sense. All other professions have a 'polluter pays' principle. Here it is the other way round; here it is we who must bring the money to compensate for the fact that the rest of us should not suffer health and environmental damage. I think it is high time that we set up the financial support systems we have for agriculture to achieve more sustainable agriculture. In fact, as the rapporteur of the alternative pointed out, we can have farming systems where the age of weaning is at a more sustainable level, for example. 7 weeks. This is the case in organic farming. It is clear that a shift to sustainable agriculture, which is not a threat to the environment and health, will take time and it will cost money. But it also costs a great deal of money to have the agriculture we have today. It is in many ways a deficit business. Too many farmers are in a totally impossible situation, for example. also because there have been banks which, up to the crisis, have completely unscrupulously speculated on borrowing money to buy land where prices rose and rose. We need to take action not only in economic terms, but also in environmental and regulatory terms, in order to achieve sustainable agriculture in the future.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510192737	The debate is open. First, the Minister for the Environment and Food. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510192818	Thank you for that, President. And thank you to the Enhedslisten, the Alternative and the SF for the motion for a resolution which allows us to discuss the future of Danish fishing and, in particular, coastal fishing. First of all, let me say that the government has no plans to terminate the vessel share agreement. So what we refer to as FKA or individual, transferable quotas, that is to say what we refer to as IOK, as the proposers would otherwise like. Since 2003 with IOK and from 2007 with the FKA, the existing quota regulation system has brought about a development of fisheries in the form of fewer but more efficient and more profitable vessels, as well as an improvement in earnings for the vast majority of fisheries. This was the development that the then conciliation parties, VKO, in 2005 just wanted to promote with the FKA system and new regulation. At the same time, the trend has led to a greater concentration of quota shares on fewer vessels. Of course, all quotas should not end up being owned by a tiny group of money-intensive fishermen, and therefore the new regulation agreement in 2005 already introduced rules to restrict the concentration of quota shares. The current so-called concentration rules are from 2012, and the government is currently reviewing the concentration rules. One of the objectives of introducing marketable quota shares was to allow fishermen to organise their fishing activities more appropriately. With the current system, fishermen can decide when they want to fish. They will not be forced to fish just to avoid other fishermen coming first, as they had to before the introduction of negotiable quotas when there was rationing. The abolition of the current system at 8 years' notice will slow down the development and investment of new modern vessels, including: because banks would probably be very reluctant to lend to vessel owners whose quotas had been cancelled. The Government therefore considers that the current system of transferable quotas has complied with the intended intentions and that there is no need for a fundamental change in the system. The fisheries service check that we are currently carrying out must include: look at whether there is a need for adjustments to elements of the current system. One of the things we will be looking at is whether there is a need for measures to support inshore fishing. With regard to the proposalers' desire to introduce measures to strengthen the nature-friendly fishing industry, the Government agrees that in the future there must also be a basis for coastal fishing, where smaller vessels help to safeguard the fishing industry in the smaller ports. They also contribute positively to a living port environment for the benefit of local communities and the tourist industry. In addition, the proposers have a proposal to introduce a labelling scheme for fish caught with natural gear. In addition, I note that in 2014 the previous government produced a consultant report on a label for gentle inshore fishing, which concluded that the cost of such a label would not match the expected impact. That is not to say that the current arrangements cannot be changed. I can inform you that I have launched an evaluation of the existing three-year coastal fishing scheme, which is part of the fisheries service check. The evaluation shall include: shall be discussed as to whether there is a need to allocate more quantities, more fish to the scheme and what other conditions should be included in the scheme. Once the evaluation has been completed, I will come up with a proposal for a future coastal fishing scheme which provides incentives for individual inshore fishermen, whether they use nets, trawls, pots or other gear, in order to strengthen coastal fishing across the board. In addition, the proposers want to introduce a system of deductions so that the first sale of quotas to a fisherman fishing naturally will be exempt from the tax on income. That proposal cannot be supported by the government. The application of the tax system to quota trading would actually amount to granting subsidies to a group of vessels and would probably have to be approved by the European Commission as State aid and would involve a reduced tax revenue. At the same time, the proposal will impose significant administrative burdens on the public sector, including: ensure that it is a first sale of a quota. Overall, I have no doubt that the current system of transferable allowances is the right system and I have no plans to terminate it. However, I am not opposed to concrete measures to strengthen coastal fishing. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution. But let me just add that it would have been a pleasure if the authors of the proposals had actually attended the Fisheries Summit where, as far as I am aware, all the members of the committee were invited. There was, of course, an opportunity to discuss such matters and also to hear what the fisheries themselves had of intentions. It is very good to take these motions for resolutions one by one, as Minister I have great respect for the fact that Members of the Danish Parliament are able to make a statement, but there is also a reality of work involving the fishing industry. We have, among other things. discussed at a major fish summit in Vejle, where there had been ample opportunity for among other things. the proposers to enter into discussions. It could also be that it had qualified the realistic to make these changes, also in relation to whether there is a real demand for changes in conditions on the part of the fishing industry.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510200319	Thank you for that, President. In the course of the debate on motion for a resolution B 152 the Left, of course, will vote against the motion. In the Left we are not interested in abolishing the system, as we see that it works as intended by increasing the quality, exploitation and competitiveness of Danish fishing. The abolition of the current system would mean that fishermen cannot provide security in their quotas for bank loans, which would affect all fishing vessels. After all, the scheme, which was created in relation to the transferable quotas, was designed to allow fishermen who had been involved in fishing for many years to withdraw from the industry so that the fishermen who were left in the industry could have profitable fishing. Those fishermen who, at the end of the day, could hardly stretch their fingers out because of hard fishing could also withdraw from the industry with their skin on their noses and properly. This is one of the reasons why we welcome the scheme we implemented, and it is also one of the reasons why Danish fishing welcomes the scheme we have implemented. It is only fair and natural for fishermen to have the opportunity to negotiate quotas so that we achieve a distribution that leads to a dynamic market. This also avoids fishermen flocking to the same fishing opportunities, that is to say what we call Olympic fishing. We cannot ignore the fact that it is most often the large vessels that fish the most and most modern and efficient. That is where we have the best working environment, and it is also where learning is most attractive if you want to make a career in fisheries, which we happily see a growing trend towards. This, of course, also has something to do with the working environment on board the vessels. So we are not promoting this by putting all Danish fishing back to the times of blue cutters. There are many advantages to the model we have knitted together today. We also say no to a new labelling system in the Left, but everyone must be free to set up one if they want to set up a labelling system. Of course, we will not stand in the way of this, but it will not be a state-funded labelling system. One of the reasons why we are also saying no to the motion for a resolution here is, of course, as the Minister explained, that we are in the process of providing a service which will have to look at Danish fisheries widely. This is everything from fishing for human consumption to industrial fishing, from coastal fishing to generational change, etc. That is why it is, of course, one of the things we look forward to in the Left, and we welcome the fact that the work is being set in motion. So in these words, on the left, I would like to reject the support for this motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510202800	Thank you to the rapporteur. There does not seem to be any brief comments, so we move on to Mrs Lisbeth Bech Poulsen, SF.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510202814	In 2007, the VK government, together with other parties in the Folketing, privatised a common good: the fish in Danish waters. After that, the very right to fish a commodity has become a speculative object, in which rights have been rented or sold to the highest bidder, who is already the richest fishermen, with large banks in the back, ready to outbid smaller fishermen at all costs. Today there are too few buyers for more expensive quotas, and it costs local communities where larger and larger vessels are served by ever fewer hands. 100 ports are closed or halved and it is almost impossible for young fishermen to enter the market. To earn the same as a primary school teacher, you have to buy quotas for 30-40 million. kr. There has also been a halving of jobs since 2000. The quota system has resulted in an environmental and social disaster that is in the process of killing the Danish fishing culture, while a few have become unimaginably rich. Quota purchases at the end of the year result in a very low or no tax payment. That is also why the SF has tabled this motion for a resolution together with the Enhedslisten and the Alternative. This is something that we think is very important. One can, of course, be concerned to varying degrees about the marine environment, but I do not believe that there are any parties in here who are completely indifferent to the fact that there are communities withering away, that there are fish that are not being landed locally, that there are secondary industries that crumble. So this is a complete, excuse me, predictable result of the privatisation and capitalisation of the quota system, because that would have been self-evident. Now I'm not gonna sing like that, that's how capitalism is up here, but when you make negotiable quotas, you can tell yourself that they concentrate on fewer and fewer hands. For those who have a lot of money and large vessels, they also have access to capital in the banks, which is why they can outbid small fishermen. And it has become virtually impossible for young fishermen or young people who want to enter the industry without capital in their backs to get in unless they have to become some kind of day owners on the vessels. So this is really, really a pity, and I really do not think that was the intention in 2007 when it was privatised. I think the intention was to save a fishing industry that was really challenged, but in the process the baby was thrown out with the bathwater. There are some who have become very, very rich on some very, very large vessels and peace be with it if it did not mean that there are communities withering and that it is impossible to get fresh blood to an industry where people would like to be master of their own boat, so to speak. That's too bad. If you voted in favour of this resolution, it would not mean that there were no bottom trawls anymore, if that is what you want, or large vessels. This would mean that over a long period of eight years we would switch the industry and say: we are doing away with this; we must of course be able to fish, but the quotas belong to society because the fish that are in the Danish waters belong to the Danes. Then you get a quota which is, of course, geared to what you have of vessels and so on, but that is only going in the wrong direction with the system we have today. There are not many years to go, so few people are sitting on incredibly large wealth, and the communities that are so dependent on fish being landed locally that there are secondary industries to take over that people can see that their sons and perhaps even daughters can come out and sail because it is a local culture. It is a Danish culture that we are throwing out because the wrong solution was chosen for a real problem in 2007. So if you do not want to support this resolution, I hope you will come up with some concrete and real proposals on what to do differently, otherwise we will not have a Danish fishing culture in a few years' time.

So they have come up with a concept where some people are running up fish, giving 20%. more than the fishermen are usually used to, and then distribute them to Copenhagen so they do not have to get past a fish auction on the West Coast. I really think this is a way of promoting quality and promoting proximity in food that I think is urgently needed. In the debate here it has also been suggested that this coastal fishing could be compared with ecology, and it is perhaps part of the same movement. There is a movement in society where consumers want quality and something where you know the whole story behind a food and that it is not just a bulk product, not a fish stick in a cardboard box, you can buy frozen in the supermarket, but that it is something where you can see where it comes from and where there is a higher traceability. If that traceability can be achieved without a state-controlled fishing brand, then that is actually fine. You can work on that. But if you have brands that are state-controlled like the E-mark, there are some other options. I think that we have seen quota concentrations and heard stories of circumventing the intentions of the rules, so that family members buy quotas and have a company on their own and do not fish the quota themselves, may have a dinghy on paper, and we can see in the accounts that there are no wage costs in the fishing company, while in that sense there is a requirement for those who have the fishing quota to operate. So there is some circumvention which we have had uncovered here in the last six months and which something needs to be done about. If that quota concentration is to take place, it must at least be done in a lawful manner. So I think that the debate here has revealed some facts and I think there is much work to be done to adjust the current quota system. ParlaMint-DK_20160510205207	The debate is open. Minister for the Environment and Food.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510205247	Thank you for that, President. I would also like to thank the authors of the motions for resolutions B 154 and B 155. Let me begin by stating that I do not agree with the proposers that there is a need to prohibit the sale and use of glyphose-containing pesticides for private use, for professional use or for use on public land. The authorisation of pesticides in Denmark is subject to an effective and restrictive authorisation system which ensures that only pesticides which do not pose an unacceptable risk to human health, to the environment or to groundwater are authorised. Let me remind you that the specially restrictive authorisation system has been agreed in the current pesticide strategy for 2013: an agreement of which the proposers are part. I do not agree with banning the sale and use of all herbicides with the active substance glyfosate for private use. The Government relies on the Danish Environmental Protection Agency's assessment that EFSA's conclusion on glyphosate is very robust and that glyphosate is not in itself carcinogenic, does not harm genetics or pose any other health hazard. The government therefore supports the re-approval of glyphosat in the EU. When it comes to the approval of glyphosate-containing pesticides in Denmark, our authorisation system ensures that no pesticides are authorised which pose an unacceptable risk to human health, the environment or groundwater. Excipients that are problematic, as if they are e.g. is carcinogenic or harmful to foetuses or reproduction, should not be found in pesticides. That is why we support the ban on the problematic excipient tallowamine, which is present in some glyphosate-containing pesticides, at the forthcoming re-evaluation of glyphosate sprays. During the review, the Danish EPA will ensure that humans are not exposed to harmful amounts of pesticides, either by direct use of pesticides or by ingestion of the substance through food and drinking water. Finally, rules on the sale of pesticides to private individuals ensure that consumers are given advice and guidance on safe use. For pesticides sold to private individuals, the aim is also to sell funds that are ready for use, including: to ensure correct use, including correct dosage. In addition to the safe use of anti-glycosate pesticides as regards human health, safe use has also been shown for the environment and groundwater. In relation to groundwater, glyphate is one of the most studied substances in the early warning system for leaching pesticides into groundwater. There have been only a few transient findings of the limit value. The Danish EPA also estimates that the approved applications in so-called fortified areas such as roads and pavements do not pose a risk to groundwater. The Commission has issued a new proposal for the re-approval of glyphosat, which will be discussed at a committee meeting on 18 and 19 May. The proposal responds to the request for a shorter period of approval and for the approval of glyfossets to be reviewed in the EU if another classification comes from ECHA. I hope that agreement can be reached and that the proposal will be put to the vote at the sitting. Against this background, the government rejects both resolutions.

ParlaMint-DK_20160510210104	Thank you very much. Today we are debating two motions for resolutions on the active substance glyphosate. In parallel, the European Food Safety Authority, EFSA, is currently assessing the substance and their latest report shows that glyphosate is not a carcinogen. So there is disagreement about glyphosate, so the European Commission has dealt with it and asked that IARC's assessment be included in the EFSA's overall assessment basis. This ensures that the assessment of glyphosate is as thorough as possible. This is essential as EU countries have to decide whether to extend the approval of glyphate. In this respect, the Socialists have stressed in the past that the substance should not be authorised until the perfect assessment basis is clear. The Enhedslisten wants us to act solely on the basis of the IARC report and to prohibit the substance. The Socialists take this matter very seriously. We care about the health of the Danes, and we do not want to gamble with the well-being of the Danes. That is why, during our reign, we took the initiative of a pesticides strategy aimed precisely at ensuring a more sound use of pesticides. This strategy expires at the end of the year and a new strategy is needed. It is essential for the Socialists that the European Union does not approve glyphobes on an imperfect basis. Similarly, we do not think that the Folketing should take a position on glyphosat until we have the scientific basis fully in place. In this context, it is also essential to state that the current rules ensure that no pesticides are sold to private people who are categorized as carcinogenic in their final solution. We think it is obvious that we are addressing the issue of glyphate in the negotiations on a new pesticide strategy, where it is our hope that the government will call for broad negotiations so that we can have a broad agreement that ensures that Danes' health is given the highest priority. Hopefully, then, we will also have a better picture of the risks associated with glyphosate. Against this background, the Socialists do not wish to support the Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution at this stage. The Danish professor at the Department of Public Health at the University of Copenhagen, Lisbeth E. Knudsen, says: "I have no doubt that there is a cancer risk in glyphosate and as a toxicologist I am concerned that we are not taken more seriously when we warn against such substances. The study is a little like an earlier study from Germany, as the Minister also mentioned in his speech, which has shown that almost all Germans have glyphobes in their bodies, and that 75%. have a concentration at least five times higher than the limit value for glyfosate in drinking water. So what is very clear, apart from the fact that there is this great scientific disagreement, how many cancer doctors think it is a carcinogenic substance, is that it is a widely used spray, which is very common in the body of children and adults. So the question is, and that is the political decision: who is the choice? And there I just have to say that I think there are a lot of Danes who think it is a little strange when the Danish People's Party stands on the floor of the Danish Parliament and says that you will not intervene against Roundup, that you will not allow this doubt to benefit children and adult Danes because you are afraid to put agriculture at a disadvantage in terms of competition. I think that for a lot of people it seems like a very bizarre balance for reasons here. I think it is important to emphasise that agriculture does not need to produce pesticides. Agriculture can produce completely without pesticides, as we see in organic farming. Even private people do not need spray poisons in their own gardens, you can fight weeds in other ways. When we think that these proposals have been important to put forward and important to debate today, it is because it is our view that this is the SF's view and I am grateful for the positive reception of the proposal and it is the view of the Alternative that such uncertainty about the risk of dangerous chemicals must benefit the health of the population at all times. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20160510214740	Thank you. There are no brief comments and no more have asked for the floor, so the debate is closed. I propose that the proposal be referred to the Committee on the Environment and Food. Are there any comments? It's agreed.
